From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

1898 conflict between Spain and the United States

This article is about the conflict between Spain and the United States. For the series of conflicts fought between Spain and its American colonies, see [Spanish American wars of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_American_wars_of_independence "Spanish American wars of independence").

**Spanish–American War[&#8202;](#ref-back-alternative_names-0)[\[a\]](#refnote-alternative_names)**

| Part of the [decolonization of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonization_of_the_Americas "Decolonization of the Americas"), <br> the [Cuban War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_War_of_Independence "Cuban War of Independence"), <br> the [Philippine Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution "Philippine Revolution"), <br> the [Territorial Expansion of the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_evolution_of_the_United_States "Territorial evolution of the United States"), <br> and [Manifest Destiny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifest_Destiny "Manifest Destiny") |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Collage_infobox_for_Spanish-American_War.jpg/330px-Collage_infobox_for_Spanish-American_War.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Collage_infobox_for_Spanish-American_War.jpg)<br>(clockwise from top left) <br>- [Signal Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Signal_Corps_(United_States_Army) "Signal Corps (United States Army)") extending telegraph lines<br>- [USS _Iowa_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Iowa_(BB-4) "USS Iowa (BB-4)") at the Victory Fleet Review in August 1898 in [New York Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Harbor "New York Harbor")<br>- Filipino soldiers wearing Spanish [pith helmets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pith_helmet "Pith helmet") outside [Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila "Manila")<br>- The Spanish signing the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1898) "Treaty of Paris (1898)")<br>- [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt "Theodore Roosevelt") and his [Rough Riders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rough_Riders "Rough Riders") at [San Juan Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_San_Juan_Hill "Battle of San Juan Hill")<br>- Replacing of the [Spanish flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Spain "Flag of Spain") at [Fort San Antonio Abad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_San_Antonio_Abad "Fort San Antonio Abad") (Fort Malate) in [Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila "Manila") |  |  |
|  | Date | April 21[&#8202;](#ref-back-8-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-8) – August 13,[&#8202;](#ref-back-16-0)[\[d\]](#refnote-16) 1898<br>(3months, 3weeks and 1day) |
| Location | - [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuba "Cuba") and [Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rico "Puerto Rico") ( [Caribbean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribbean_Sea "Caribbean Sea"))<br>- [Guam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guam "Guam") ( [Pacific Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Ocean "Pacific Ocean")) and the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines") ( [Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia "Southeast Asia")) |  |
| Result | American victory |  |
| Territorial<br>changes | Spain relinquishes sovereignty over [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuba "Cuba") effectively recognizing US occupation; the United States annexes the territories of [Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rico "Puerto Rico"), [Guam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guam "Guam"), and the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines"). ($20million paid to Spain by the United States for the Spanish infrastructure). |  |
| Belligerents |  |  |

Port-au-Prince (1919)")<br>- [2nd Port-au-Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Port-au-Prince_(1920) "Battle of Port-au-Prince (1920)")<br>[Dominican Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_the_Dominican_Republic_(1916%E2%80%931924) "United States occupation of the Dominican Republic (1916–1924)")<br>- [Santo Domingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santo_Domingo_Affair "Santo Domingo Affair")<br>- [Puerto Plata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Puerto_Plata "Battle of Puerto Plata")<br>- [Las Trencheras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Las_Trencheras "Battle of Las Trencheras")<br>- [Guayacanas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Guayacanas "Battle of Guayacanas")<br>- [San Francisco de Macoris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_San_Francisco_de_Macoris "Battle of San Francisco de Macoris") |

The **Spanish–American War**[&#8202;](#ref-back-alternative_names-1)[\[a\]](#refnote-alternative_names) (April 21 – August 13, 1898) was an armed conflict fought between the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States") and [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain "Spain") in 1898. It began with the sinking of the [USS _Maine_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Maine_(1890) "USS Maine (1890)") in [Havana Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Harbor "Havana Harbor") in [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Cuba "Captaincy General of Cuba"), and eventually resulted in the U.S. acquiring [Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rico "Puerto Rico"), [Guam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guam "Guam"), the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_the_Philippines "Commonwealth of the Philippines"), and [establishing a protectorate over Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platt_Amendment "Platt Amendment"). It represented U.S. intervention in the [Cuban War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_War_of_Independence "Cuban War of Independence") and [Philippine Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution "Philippine Revolution"), with the latter later leading to the [Philippine–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine%E2%80%93American_War "Philippine–American War"). The Spanish–American War brought an end to almost four centuries of Spanish presence in the Americas, Asia, and the Pacific; the United States meanwhile not only became a major world power, but also gained several island possessions spanning the globe, which provoked rancorous debate over the wisdom of [expansionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansionism "Expansionism").[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-32)

The 19th century represented a clear decline for the [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire "Spanish Empire"), while the United States went from a newly founded country to a rising colonial power. In 1895, Cuban nationalists [began a revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_War_of_Independence "Cuban War of Independence") against [Spanish colonial rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Cuba "Captaincy General of Cuba"), which was brutally suppressed by the Spanish colonial authorities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-historian-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-historian) W. Joseph Campbell argued that [yellow journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_journalism "Yellow journalism") in the U.S. exaggerated the Spanish atrocities in Cuba to sell more newspapers and magazines,[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-34) which swayed American public opinion in support of the Cuban rebels. But historian [Andrea Pitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrea_Pitzer "Andrea Pitzer") also points to the actual shift toward savagery of the Spanish military leadership, who adopted the brutal [reconcentration policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconcentration_policy "Reconcentration policy") after replacing the relatively conservative Governor-General of Cuba [Arsenio Martínez Campos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenio_Mart%C3%ADnez_Campos "Arsenio Martínez Campos") with the more unscrupulous and aggressive [Valeriano Weyler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valeriano_Weyler "Valeriano Weyler"), nicknamed "The Butcher."[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-35)[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-36) President [Grover Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Cleveland "Grover Cleveland") resisted mounting demands for U.S. intervention, as did his successor [William McKinley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_McKinley "William McKinley").[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-37) Though not seeking a war, McKinley made preparations in readiness for one.

In January 1898, the [U.S. Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Navy "United States Navy") armored cruiser USS _Maine_ was sent to [Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana "Havana") to provide protection for U.S. citizens. After the _Maine_ was sunk by a mysterious explosion in the harbor on February 15, 1898, political pressures pushed McKinley to receive congressional authority to use military force. On April 21, the U.S. began a blockade of Cuba,[&#8202;](#ref-back-trask57-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-trask57) and soon after Spain and the U.S. declared war. The war was fought in both the Caribbean and the Pacific, where American war advocates correctly anticipated that U.S. naval power would prove decisive. On May 1, a squadron of U.S. warships [destroyed the Spanish fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_Bay "Battle of Manila Bay") at Manila Bay in the Philippines and captured the harbor. The first U.S. forces landed in Cuba on June 10 in the island's southeast, moving west and engaging in the [Battles of El Caney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_El_Caney "Battle of El Caney") and [San Juan Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_San_Juan_Hill "Battle of San Juan Hill") on July 1 and then destroying the Spanish fleet and capturing Santiago de Cuba on July 17.[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-38) On June 20, the island of [Guam surrendered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Guam_(1898) "Capture of Guam (1898)") without resistance, and on July 25, U.S. troops [landed on Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rico_campaign "Puerto Rico campaign"), of which a blockade had begun on May 8 and where fighting continued until an armistice was signed on August 13.

The war formally ended with the [1898 Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1898) "Treaty of Paris (1898)"), signed on December 10 with terms favorable to the U.S. The treaty ceded ownership of Puerto Rico, Guam, and the Philippines to the U.S., and set Cuba up to become an independent state in 1902, although in practice it became a U.S. protectorate. The cession of the Philippines involved payment of $20million ($660million in 2025) to Spain by the U.S. to cover infrastructure owned by Spain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-39) In Spain, the defeat in the war was a profound shock to the national psyche and provoked a thorough philosophical and artistic reevaluation of Spanish society known as the [Generation of '98](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_of_'98 "Generation of '98").[&#8202;](#ref-back-108–109-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-108–109)

## Historical background

### Spain's attitude towards its colonies

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/World_1898_empires_colonies_territory.png/330px-World_1898_empires_colonies_territory.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:World_1898_empires_colonies_territory.png) World empires and colonies 1898. In yellow Spain and in light blue United States.

The combined problems arising from the [Peninsular War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_War "Peninsular War") (1807–1814), the loss of most of its [colonies in the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonization_of_the_Americas "Spanish colonization of the Americas") in the early 19th-century [Spanish American wars of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_American_wars_of_independence "Spanish American wars of independence"), and three [Carlist Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlist_Wars "Carlist Wars") (1832–1876) marked a low point for Spanish colonialism.[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-41) Liberal Spanish elites like [Antonio Cánovas del Castillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_C%C3%A1novas_del_Castillo "Antonio Cánovas del Castillo") and [Emilio Castelar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_Castelar "Emilio Castelar") offered new interpretations of the concept of "empire" to dovetail with the emerging [Spanish nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_nationalism "Spanish nationalism"). Cánovas made clear in an address to the [University of Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complutense_University_of_Madrid "Complutense University of Madrid") in 1882[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-42)[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-43) his view of the Spanish nation as based on shared cultural and linguistic elements—on both sides of the Atlantic—that tied Spain's territories together.

Cánovas saw Spanish colonialism as more "benevolent" than that of other European colonial powers. The prevalent opinion in Spain before the war regarded the spreading of " [civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilization "Civilization")" and [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity "Christianity") as Spain's main objective and contribution to the [New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World "New World"). The concept of cultural unity bestowed special significance on Cuba, which had been Spanish for almost four hundred years, and was viewed as an integral part of the Spanish nation. The focus on preserving the empire would have negative consequences for Spain's national pride in the aftermath of the Spanish–American War.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-44)

### American interest in the Caribbean

In 1823, the fifth American President [James Monroe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Monroe "James Monroe") (1758–1831, served 1817–25) enunciated the [Monroe Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine "Monroe Doctrine"), which stated that the United States would not tolerate further efforts by European governments to retake or expand their colonial holdings in the [Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americas "Americas") or to interfere with the newly independent states in the hemisphere. The U.S. would, however, respect the status of the existing European colonies. Before the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War "American Civil War") (1861–1865), Southern interests attempted to have the United States purchase [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Cuba "Captaincy General of Cuba") and convert it into a new [slave state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_state "Slave state"). The pro-slavery element proposed the [Ostend Manifesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostend_Manifesto "Ostend Manifesto") of 1854. Anti-slavery forces rejected it.

After the American Civil War and Cuba's [Ten Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_Years'_War "Ten Years' War"), U.S. businessmen began monopolizing the devalued sugar markets in Cuba. In 1894, 90% of Cuba's total exports went to the United States, which also provided 40% of Cuba's imports.[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-45) Cuba's total exports to the U.S. were almost twelve times larger than the export to Spain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-46) U.S. business interests indicated that while Spain still held political authority over Cuba, it was the U.S. that held economic power over Cuba.

The U.S. became interested in a trans-isthmus canal in either [Nicaragua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaragua "Nicaragua") or [Panama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama "Panama") and realized the need for naval protection. Captain [Alfred Thayer Mahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Thayer_Mahan "Alfred Thayer Mahan") was an exceptionally influential theorist; his ideas were much admired by future 26th President [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt "Theodore Roosevelt"), as the U.S. rapidly built a powerful [naval fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Navy "United States Navy") of steel warships in the 1880s and 1890s. Roosevelt served as [Assistant Secretary of the Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assistant_Secretary_of_the_Navy "Assistant Secretary of the Navy") from 1897 to 1898 and was an aggressive supporter of an American war with Spain over Cuban interests.

Meanwhile, the "Cuba Libre" movement, led by Cuban intellectual [José Martí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mart%C3%AD "José Martí") until he died in 1895, had established offices in Florida.[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-47) The face of the Cuban revolution in the U.S. was the " [Cuban Junta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Junta "Cuban Junta")", under the leadership of [Tomás Estrada Palma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom%C3%A1s_Estrada_Palma "Tomás Estrada Palma"), who in 1902 became Cuba's first president. The Junta dealt with leading newspapers and Washington officials and held fund-raising events across the U.S. It funded and smuggled weapons. It mounted an extensive propaganda campaign that generated enormous popular support in the U.S. in favor of the Cubans. Protestant churches and most Democrats were supportive, but business interests called on Washington to negotiate a settlement and avoid war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-48)

Cuba attracted enormous American attention, but almost no discussion involved the other Spanish colonies of [Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rico "Puerto Rico"), also in the Caribbean, or of the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines") or [Guam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guam "Guam").[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-49) Historians note that there was no popular demand in the United States for an overseas colonial empire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-50)

## Path to war

### Cuban struggle for independence

Main article: [Cuban War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_War_of_Independence "Cuban War of Independence")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/La_fallera_de_l%27oncle_Sam.JPG/250px-La_fallera_de_l%27oncle_Sam.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:La_fallera_de_l'oncle_Sam.JPG) A [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaniards "Spaniards") propaganda drawing published in _[La Campana de Gràcia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Campana_de_Gr%C3%A0cia "La Campana de Gràcia")_ (1896) by [Manuel Moliné](https://ca.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel%20Molin%C3%A9%20i%20Muns "ca:Manuel Moliné i Muns") criticizing U.S. behavior regarding Cuba. Upper text (in old [Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language "Catalan language")) reads: "Uncle Sam's craving", and below: "To keep the island so it won't get lost".

The first serious bid for Cuban independence was the Ten Years' War, which erupted in 1868 and was subdued by the authorities a decade later. Neither the fighting nor the reforms in the [Pact of Zanjón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pact_of_Zanj%C3%B3n "Pact of Zanjón") (February 1878) quelled the desire of some revolutionaries for wider autonomy and, ultimately, independence. One such revolutionary, José Martí, continued to promote Cuban financial and political freedom in exile. In early 1895, after years of organizing, Martí launched a three-pronged invasion of the island.[&#8202;](#ref-back-books.google.com-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-books.google.com)

The plan called for one group from [Santo Domingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santo_Domingo "Santo Domingo") in the [Dominican Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Republic "Dominican Republic") led by [Máximo Gómez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A1ximo_G%C3%B3mez "Máximo Gómez"), one group from [Costa Rica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa_Rica "Costa Rica") led by [Antonio Maceo Grajales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Maceo_Grajales "Antonio Maceo Grajales"), and another from the United States (preemptively thwarted by U.S. officials in Florida) to land in different places on the island and provoke an uprising. While their call for revolution, the _grito de Baire_, was successful, the result was not the grand show of force Martí had expected. With a quick victory effectively lost, the revolutionaries settled in to fight a protracted guerrilla campaign.[&#8202;](#ref-back-books.google.com-1)[\[43\]](#refnote-books.google.com)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/1896-03-28%2C_Blanco_y_Negro%2C_El_general_Weyler_y_las_autoridades_de_La_Habana.jpg/250px-1896-03-28%2C_Blanco_y_Negro%2C_El_general_Weyler_y_las_autoridades_de_La_Habana.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1896-03-28,_Blanco_y_Negro,_El_general_Weyler_y_las_autoridades_de_La_Habana.jpg) General Weyler and the authorities of Havana

Antonio Cánovas del Castillo, the architect of Spain's Restoration constitution and the prime minister at the time, ordered General [Arsenio Martínez-Campos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arsenio_Mart%C3%ADnez-Campos "Arsenio Martínez-Campos"), a distinguished veteran of the war against the previous uprising in Cuba, to quell the revolt. Campos's reluctance to accept his new assignment and his method of containing the revolt to the province of [Oriente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriente_Province "Oriente Province") earned him criticism in the Spanish press.[&#8202;](#ref-back-online-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-online)
The mounting pressure forced Cánovas to replace General Campos with General [Valeriano Weyler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valeriano_Weyler "Valeriano Weyler"), a soldier who had experience in quelling rebellions in overseas provinces and the Spanish metropole. Weyler deprived the insurgency of weaponry, supplies, and assistance by ordering the residents of some Cuban districts to move to [reconcentration areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconcentration_policy "Reconcentration policy") near the military headquarters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-online-1)[\[44\]](#refnote-online) This strategy was effective in slowing the spread of rebellion. In the United States, this fueled the fire of anti-Spanish propaganda.[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-53) In a political speech, President [William McKinley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_McKinley "William McKinley") used this to ram Spanish actions against armed rebels. He even said this "was not civilized warfare" but "extermination".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Annual_Message-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-Annual_Message)[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-55)

### Spanish attitude

Spain depended on Cuba for prestige and trade, and used it as a training ground for its army. [Spanish Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Prime_Minister "Spanish Prime Minister") Antonio Cánovas del Castillo announced that "the Spanish nation is disposed to sacrifice to the last peseta of its treasure and to the last drop of blood of the last Spaniard before consenting that anyone snatch from it even one piece of its territory".[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-56) He had long dominated and stabilized Spanish politics. He was assassinated in 1897 by Italian anarchist [Michele Angiolillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michele_Angiolillo "Michele Angiolillo"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-57) leaving a Spanish political system that was not stable and could not risk a blow to its prestige.[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-58)

### US response

Further information: [Presidency of William McKinley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_William_McKinley "Presidency of William McKinley")

The eruption of the Cuban revolt, Weyler's measures, and the popular fury these events whipped up proved to be a boon to the newspaper industry in New York City. [Joseph Pulitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Pulitzer "Joseph Pulitzer") of the _[New York World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_World "New York World")_ and [William Randolph Hearst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Randolph_Hearst "William Randolph Hearst") of the _[New York Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Journal "New York Journal")_ recognized the potential for great headlines and stories that would sell copies. Both papers denounced Spain but had little influence outside New York. American opinion generally saw Spain as a hopelessly backward power that was unable to deal fairly with Cuba. American Catholics were divided before the war began but supported it enthusiastically once it started.[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-59)[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-60)

The U.S. had important economic interests that were being harmed by the prolonged conflict and deepening uncertainty about Cuba's future. Shipping firms that had relied heavily on trade with Cuba now suffered losses as the conflict continued unresolved.[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-61) These firms pressed Congress and McKinley to seek an end to the revolt. Other American business concerns, specifically those who had invested in Cuban sugar, looked to the Spanish to restore order.[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-62) Stability, not war, was the goal of both interests. How stability would be achieved would depend largely on the ability of Spain and the U.S. to work out their issues diplomatically.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Judge-2-6-1897.jpg/250px-Judge-2-6-1897.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Judge-2-6-1897.jpg) An American cartoon published in _[Judge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judge_(magazine) "Judge (magazine)")_, February 6, 1897: [Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_(name) "Columbia (name)") (representing the American people) reaches out to the oppressed Cuba (the caption under the chained child reads "Spain's 16th century methods") while [Uncle Sam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncle_Sam "Uncle Sam") (representing the U.S. government) sits blindfolded, refusing to see the atrocities or use his guns to intervene (cartoon by [Grant E. Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grant_E._Hamilton "Grant E. Hamilton")).

Lieutenant Commander Charles Train, in 1894, in his preparatory notes in an outlook of an armed conflict between Spain and the United States, wrote that Cuba was entirely dependent on the outside world for food supplies, coal, and maritime supplies and that Spain would not be able to resupply a naval expeditionary force locally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto1-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-auto1)

While tension increased among the Cubans and Spanish government, popular support of intervention began to spring up in the United States. Many Americans likened the Cuban revolt to the American Revolution, and they viewed the Spanish government as a tyrannical oppressor. Historian Louis Pérez notes that "The proposition of war in behalf of Cuban independence took hold immediately and held on thereafter. Such was the sense of the public mood." Many poems and songs were written in the United States to express support of the "Cuba Libre" movement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-64) At the same time, many [African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans "African Americans"), facing growing racial discrimination and increasing retardation of their civil rights, wanted to take part in the war. They saw it as a way to advance the cause of equality, hoping that service to the country would help to gain political and public respect from the wider population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-65)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/USS_Maine_entering_Havana_harbor_HD-SN-99-01929.JPEG/250px-USS_Maine_entering_Havana_harbor_HD-SN-99-01929.JPEG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:USS_Maine_entering_Havana_harbor_HD-SN-99-01929.JPEG) The battleship _Maine_ entering [Havana Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Harbor "Havana Harbor")

President McKinley, well aware of the political complexity surrounding the conflict, wanted to end the revolt peacefully. He began to negotiate with the Spanish government, hoping that the talks would dampen yellow journalism in the United States and soften support for war with Spain. An attempt was made to negotiate a peace before McKinley took office. However, the Spanish refused to take part in the negotiations. In 1897 McKinley appointed [Stewart L. Woodford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stewart_L._Woodford "Stewart L. Woodford") as the new minister to Spain, who again offered to negotiate a peace. In October 1897, the Spanish government refused the United States' offer to negotiate between the Spanish and the Cubans, but promised the U.S. it would give the Cubans more autonomy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-:0) However, with the election of a more liberal Spanish government in November, Spain began to change its policies in Cuba. First, the new Spanish government told the United States that it was willing to offer a change in the Reconcentration policies if the Cuban rebels agreed to a cessation of hostilities. This time the rebels refused the terms in hopes that continued conflict would lead to U.S. intervention and the creation of an independent Cuba.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-1)[\[58\]](#refnote-:0) The liberal Spanish government also recalled the Spanish Governor-General Valeriano Weyler from Cuba. This action alarmed many Cubans loyal to Spain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-:1)

The Cubans loyal to Weyler began planning large demonstrations to take place when the next Governor General, [Ramón Blanco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram%C3%B3n_Blanco,_1st_Marquess_of_Pe%C3%B1a_Plata "Ramón Blanco, 1st Marquess of Peña Plata"), arrived in Cuba. U.S. consul Fitzhugh Lee learned of these plans and sent a request to the U.S. State Department to send a U.S. warship to Cuba.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[59\]](#refnote-:1) This request led to the [armored cruiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armored_cruiser "Armored cruiser") [USS _Maine_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Maine_(1890) "USS Maine (1890)") being sent to Cuba. While _Maine_ was docked in Havana harbor, a spontaneous explosion sank the ship. The sinking of _Maine_ was blamed on the Spanish and made the possibility of a negotiated peace very slim.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPérez199858-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPérez199858) Throughout the negotiation process, the major European powers, especially Britain, France, and Russia, generally supported the American position and urged Spain to give in.[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-69) Spain repeatedly promised specific reforms that would pacify Cuba but failed to deliver; American patience ran out.[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-70)

### USS _Maine_ dispatch to Havana and loss

Further information: [USS Maine (1890)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Maine_(1890) "USS Maine (1890)")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Wreck_of_the_U.S.S._Maine%2C_ca._1898_-_NARA_-_512929.jpg/330px-Wreck_of_the_U.S.S._Maine%2C_ca._1898_-_NARA_-_512929.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Wreck_of_the_U.S.S._Maine,_ca._1898_-_NARA_-_512929.jpg)

The sunken [USS _Maine_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Maine_(1890) "USS Maine (1890)") in [Havana harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_harbor "Havana harbor")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/18980216_Blown_Up_By_Spain_-_USS_Maine_-_The_Evening_Times_%28Washington%2C_D.C.%29.jpg/330px-18980216_Blown_Up_By_Spain_-_USS_Maine_-_The_Evening_Times_%28Washington%2C_D.C.%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:18980216_Blown_Up_By_Spain_-_USS_Maine_-_The_Evening_Times_(Washington,_D.C.).jpg)

Though publication of a [U.S. Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Navy "United States Navy") investigation report would take a month, this [Washington D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_D.C. "Washington D.C.") newspaper[&#8202;](#ref-back-EverningTimes_18980216-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-EverningTimes_18980216) was among those asserting within one day that the explosion was not accidental.

McKinley sent [USS _Maine_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Maine_(1890) "USS Maine (1890)") to [Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana "Havana") to ensure the safety of American citizens and interests, and to underscore the urgent need for reform. Naval forces were moved in position to attack simultaneously on several fronts if the war was not avoided. As _Maine_ left Florida, a large part of the [North Atlantic Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Squadron "North Atlantic Squadron") was moved to Key West and the [Gulf of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Mexico "Gulf of Mexico"). Others were also moved just off the shore of Lisbon, and others were moved to [Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong "Hong Kong").[&#8202;](#ref-back-offner2004p56-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-offner2004p56)

At 9:40 P.M. on February 15, 1898, _Maine_ sank in [Havana Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana_Harbor "Havana Harbor") after suffering a massive explosion. More than 3/4 of the ship's crew of 355 sailors, officers and Marines died as a result of the explosion. Of the 94 survivors only 16 were uninjured.[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-73) In total, 260[&#8202;](#ref-back-history.navy.mil-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-history.navy.mil) servicemen were killed in the initial explosion, and six more died shortly thereafter from injuries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-history.navy.mil-1)[\[66\]](#refnote-history.navy.mil)

While McKinley urged patience and did not declare that Spain had caused the explosion, the deaths of hundreds of American[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-75) sailors held the public's attention. McKinley asked Congress to appropriate $50million for defense, and Congress unanimously obliged. Most American leaders believed that the cause of the explosion was unknown. Still, public attention was now riveted on the situation and Spain could not find a diplomatic solution to avoid war. Spain appealed to the European powers, most of whom advised it to accept U.S. conditions for Cuba in order to avoid war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKeenan2001[httpsarchiveorgdetailsencyclopediaofsp00keenpage372_372]-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKeenan2001[httpsarchiveorgdetailsencyclopediaofsp00keenpage372_372]) Germany urged a united European stand against the United States but took no action.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTETucker2009[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8V3vZxOmHssCpgPA614_614]-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTETucker2009[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8V3vZxOmHssCpgPA614_614])

The U.S. Navy's investigation, made public on March 28, concluded that the ship's powder magazines were ignited when an external explosion was set off under the ship's hull. Report investigation omitted to place responsibility for the external explosion but nonetheless poured fuel on popular indignation in the U.S., making war virtually inevitable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-offner2004p57-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-offner2004p57) Spain's investigation came to the opposite conclusion: the explosion originated within the ship. Other investigations in later years came to various contradictory conclusions, but had no bearing on the coming of the war. In 1974, Admiral [Hyman George Rickover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyman_George_Rickover "Hyman George Rickover") had his staff look at the documents and decided there was an internal explosion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fisher2010-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-fisher2010) A study commissioned by _[National Geographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Geographic "National Geographic")_ magazine in 1999, using AME computer modeling, reported: "By examining the bottom plating of the ship and how it bent and folded, AME concluded that the destruction could have been caused by a mine."[&#8202;](#ref-back-fisher2010-1)[\[71\]](#refnote-fisher2010)

### Declaring war

Main article: [American propaganda of the Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_propaganda_of_the_Spanish%E2%80%93American_War "American propaganda of the Spanish–American War")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Mapa_ilustrado_del_Reino_de_Espa%C3%B1a_y_sus_posesiones_para_la_Guardia_Civil.jpg/250px-Mapa_ilustrado_del_Reino_de_Espa%C3%B1a_y_sus_posesiones_para_la_Guardia_Civil.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mapa_ilustrado_del_Reino_de_Espa%C3%B1a_y_sus_posesiones_para_la_Guardia_Civil.jpg) Illustrated map published by the [Guardia Civil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardia_Civil "Guardia Civil") showing the Kingdom of Spain and its remaining colonial possessions in 1895 ( [Caroline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Islands "Caroline Islands") and [Mariana Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariana_Islands "Mariana Islands"), as well as [Spanish Sahara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Sahara "Spanish Sahara"), [Morocco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Morocco "Spanish Morocco"), [Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Guinea "Spanish Guinea") and [Guam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guam "Guam") are not included.)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Cuban_soldiers%2C_1898.jpg/250px-Cuban_soldiers%2C_1898.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cuban_soldiers,_1898.jpg) Cuban insurgent soldiers, who had already been fighting for [Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_War_of_Independence "Cuban War of Independence") against Spain since 1895

After _Maine_ was destroyed, New York City newspaper publishers Hearst and Pulitzer decided that the Spanish were to blame, and they publicized this theory as fact in their papers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-80) Even prior to the explosion, both had published sensationalistic accounts of "atrocities" committed by the Spanish in Cuba; headlines such as "Spanish Murderers" were commonplace in their newspapers. Following the explosion, this tone escalated with the headline "Remember The Maine, To Hell with Spain!", quickly appearing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-81)[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-82) Their press exaggerated what was happening and how the Spanish were treating the Cuban prisoners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-83) The stories were based on factual accounts, but most of the time, the articles that were published were embellished and written with incendiary language causing emotional and often heated responses among readers. A common myth falsely states that when illustrator [Frederic Remington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederic_Remington "Frederic Remington") said there was no war brewing in Cuba, Hearst responded: "You furnish the pictures and I'll furnish the war."[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-84)

However, this new " [yellow journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_journalism "Yellow journalism")" was uncommon outside New York City, and historians no longer consider it the major force shaping the national mood.[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-85) Public opinion nationwide did demand immediate action, overwhelming the efforts of President McKinley, [Speaker of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives "Speaker of the United States House of Representatives") [Thomas Brackett Reed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Brackett_Reed "Thomas Brackett Reed"), and the business community to find a negotiated solution. Wall Street, big business, high finance and Main Street businesses across the country were vocally opposed to war and demanded peace.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Pratt-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-Pratt) After years of severe depression, the economic outlook for the domestic economy was suddenly bright again in 1897. However, the uncertainties of warfare posed a serious threat to full economic recovery. "War would impede the march of prosperity and put the country back many years," warned the _New Jersey Trade Review._ The leading railroad magazine editorialized, "From a commercial and mercenary standpoint it seems peculiarly bitter that this war should come when the country had already suffered so much and so needed rest and peace." McKinley paid close attention to the strong antiwar consensus of the business community, and strengthened his resolve to use diplomacy and negotiation rather than brute force to end the Spanish tyranny in Cuba.[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-87) Historian [Nick Kapur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Kapur "Nick Kapur") argues that McKinley's actions as he moved toward war were rooted not in various pressure groups but in his deeply held "Victorian" values, especially arbitration, pacifism, humanitarianism, and manly self-restraint.[&#8202;](#ref-back-88-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-88)

A speech delivered by Republican Senator [Redfield Proctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redfield_Proctor "Redfield Proctor") of Vermont on March 17, 1898, thoroughly analyzed the situation and greatly strengthened the pro-war cause. Proctor concluded that war was the only answer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cubarmy-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-cubarmy) Many in the business and religious communities which had until then opposed war, switched sides, leaving McKinley and Speaker Reed almost alone in their resistance to a war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-90)[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-91)[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-92) On April 11, McKinley ended his resistance and asked Congress for authority to send American troops to Cuba to end the civil war there, knowing that Congress would force a war.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Spanish_American_War_transport_Seneca.jpg/330px-Spanish_American_War_transport_Seneca.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Spanish_American_War_transport_Seneca.jpg) The American transport ship _Seneca_, a chartered vessel that carried troops to Puerto Rico and Cuba

On April 19, while Congress was considering [joint resolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_resolution "Joint resolution") supporting Cuban independence, Republican Senator [Henry M. Teller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_M._Teller "Henry M. Teller") of [Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado "Colorado") proposed the [Teller Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teller_Amendment "Teller Amendment") to ensure that the U.S. would not establish permanent control over Cuba after the war. The amendment, disclaiming any intention to annex Cuba, passed the Senate 42 to 35; the House concurred the same day, 311 to 6. The amended resolution demanded Spanish withdrawal and authorized the President to use as much military force as he thought necessary to help Cuba gain independence from Spain. President McKinley signed the joint resolution on April 20, 1898, and the ultimatum was sent to Spain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-stat33.738-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-stat33.738) In response, Spain severed diplomatic relations with the United States on April 21. On the same day, the U.S. Navy began a blockade of Cuba.[&#8202;](#ref-back-trask57-1)[\[6\]](#refnote-trask57)

On April 25, the U.S. Congress [responded in kind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_declaration_of_war_upon_Spain "United States declaration of war upon Spain"), declaring that a state of war between the U.S. and Spain had _[de facto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_facto "De facto")_ existed since April 21, the day the blockade of Cuba had begun.[&#8202;](#ref-back-trask57-2)[\[6\]](#refnote-trask57) It was the embodiment of the naval plan created by Lieutenant Commander Charles Train four years ago, stating once the US enacted a proclamation of war against Spain, it would mobilize its [North Atlantic Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Squadron "North Atlantic Squadron") to form an efficient blockade in Havana, [Matanzas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matanzas "Matanzas") and [Sagua La Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagua_La_Grande "Sagua La Grande").[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto1-1)[\[55\]](#refnote-auto1)

The Navy was ready, but the Army was not well-prepared for the war and made radical changes in plans and quickly purchased supplies. In the spring of 1898, the strength of the [U.S. Regular Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regular_Army_(United_States) "Regular Army (United States)") was just 24,593 soldiers. The Army wanted 50,000 new men but received over 220,000 through volunteers and the mobilization of [state National Guard units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard_(United_States) "National Guard (United States)"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-94) even gaining nearly 100,000 men on the first night after the explosion of USS _Maine_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-95)

President McKinley issued two calls for volunteers, the first on April 23 which called for 125,000 men to enlist, followed by a second appeal for a further 75,000 volunteers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-96) States in the Northeast, Midwest, and the West quickly filled their volunteer quota. In response to the surplus influx of volunteers, several Northern states had their quotas increased. Contrastingly, some Southern states struggled to fulfil even the first mandated quota, namely Alabama, Kentucky, Mississippi, North Carolina, South Carolina, and Virginia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-97) In Paducah, Kentucky, on the day war was declared, 185 men were accepted at a morning armory meeting, with the mayor arranging a brass band to escort soldiers to the depot and a cannon signal designated to call volunteers to assemble; nevertheless, genuine volunteer numbers in the state stood at only around 800 against a federal quota of 400, with the Kentucky State Guard bringing the total strength only to approximately 1,500.[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-98) In Alabama, the inspecting surgeons' rejection of a large number of original volunteers forced Colonel Higdon to issue a new call through the Birmingham Age-Herald for 100 additional men simply to bring the twelve companies of the First Alabama Regiment to their required strength of 84 men each, with recruits being dispatched from Birmingham in groups of seventeen to twenty at a time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-99)

The majority of states did not allow African-American men to volunteer, which impeded recruitment in Southern states, especially those with large African-American populations. Quota requirements, based on total population, were unmanageable, as they were disproportionate compared to the actual population permitted to volunteer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-100) This was especially evident in some states, such as Kentucky and Mississippi, which accepted out-of-state volunteers to aid in meeting their quotas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-101)

This Southern apprehension towards enlistment can also be attributed to "a war weariness derived from the Confederacy's defeat in the Civil War."[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-102) Many in the South were still recuperating financially after their losses in the Civil War, and the upcoming war did not provide much hope for economic prosperity in the South. The prospect of a naval war gave anxiety to those in the South. The financial security of those working and living in the cotton belt relied heavily upon trade across the Atlantic, which would be disrupted by a nautical war, the prospect of which fostered a reluctance to enlist.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-103) Potential volunteers were also not financially incentivized, with pay per month initially being $13.00, which then was then raised to $15.60 for combat pay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-104) It was more economically promising for most Southern men to continue in their own enterprises rather than enlist.

### Historiography

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/The_last_stand_of_the_Spanish_Garrison.jpg/250px-The_last_stand_of_the_Spanish_Garrison.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_last_stand_of_the_Spanish_Garrison.jpg) The last stand of the Spanish Garrison in Cuba by [Murat Halstead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murat_Halstead "Murat Halstead"), 1898

The overwhelming consensus of observers in the 1890s, and historians ever since, is that an upsurge of humanitarian concern with the plight of the Cubans was the main motivating force that caused the war with Spain in 1898. McKinley put it succinctly in late 1897 that if Spain failed to resolve its crisis, the United States would see "a duty imposed by our obligations to ourselves, to civilization and humanity to intervene with force".[&#8202;](#ref-back-Annual_Message-1)[\[46\]](#refnote-Annual_Message) Intervention in terms of negotiating a settlement proved impossible—neither Spain nor the insurgents would agree. Louis Perez states, "Certainly the moralistic determinants of war in 1898 has been accorded preponderant explanatory weight in the historiography."[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-105) By the 1950s, however, American political scientists began attacking the war as a mistake based on idealism, arguing that a better policy would be realism. They discredited the idealism by suggesting the people were deliberately misled by propaganda and sensationalist yellow journalism. Political scientist Robert Osgood, writing in 1953, led the attack on the American decision process as a confused mix of "self-righteousness and genuine moral fervor," in the form of a "crusade" and a combination of "knight-errantry and national self- assertiveness."[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-106) Osgood argued:

A war to free Cuba from Spanish despotism, corruption, and cruelty, from the filth and disease and barbarity of General 'Butcher' Weyler's reconcentration camps, from the devastation of haciendas, the extermination of families, and the outraging of women; that would be a blow for humanity and democracy.... No one could doubt it if he believed—and skepticism was not popular—the exaggerations of the Cuban _Junta's_ propaganda and the lurid distortions and imaginative lies pervade by the "yellow sheets" of Hearst and Pulitzer at the combined rate of 2 million \[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[newspaper copies\] a day.[\[99\]](#refnote-107)

In his _War and Empire_,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-:3) Prof. Paul Atwood of the University of Massachusetts (Boston) writes:

> The Spanish–American War was fomented on outright lies and trumped up accusations against the intended enemy.... War fever in the general population never reached a critical temperature until the accidental sinking of the _USS Maine_ was deliberately, and falsely, attributed to Spanish villainy.... In a cryptic message... Senator Lodge wrote that 'There may be an explosion any day in Cuba which would settle a great many things. We have got a battleship in the harbor of Havana, and our fleet, which overmatches anything the Spanish have, is masked at the Dry Tortugas.

In his autobiography,[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-109) Theodore Roosevelt gave his views of the origins of the war:

> Our own direct interests were great, because of the Cuban tobacco and sugar, and especially because of Cuba's relation to the projected Isthmian \[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[Panama\] Canal. But even greater were our interests from the standpoint of humanity.... It was our duty, even more from the standpoint of National honor than from the standpoint of National interest, to stop the devastation and destruction. Because of these considerations I favored war.

In the article 'Meaning of the Maine', Louis A. Perez Jr. challenges Roosevelt's assertion:

> The Maine incident fits well into a larger idealized view of a political universe. It lends itself easily to the service of validating normative democratic theory. It adds credence to commonly shared values of the national mission. This indeed may be a central if unspoken element of much of the historiography and the larger ideological role of the Maine: a plausible denial of the proposition of war as an instrument of policy-war, in short, deliberate and by design, for the purpose of territorial expansion.[\[102\]](#refnote-110)

## Pacific theater

### Philippines

See also: [Philippine Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution "Philippine Revolution")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Pacifico-98.jpg/330px-Pacifico-98.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pacifico-98.jpg) The Pacific theater of the Spanish–American War

In the 333 years of Spanish rule, the Philippines developed from a small overseas colony governed from the Mexico-based [Viceroyalty of New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viceroyalty_of_New_Spain "Viceroyalty of New Spain") to a land with modern elements in the cities. The Spanish-speaking middle classes of the 19th century were mostly educated in the liberal ideas coming from Europe. Among these [Ilustrados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilustrado "Ilustrado") was the Filipino national hero [José Rizal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Rizal "José Rizal"), who demanded larger reforms from the Spanish authorities. This movement eventually led to the [Philippine Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution "Philippine Revolution") against Spanish colonial rule. The revolution had been in a state of [truce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truce "Truce") since the signing of the [Pact of Biak-na-Bato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pact_of_Biak-na-Bato "Pact of Biak-na-Bato") in 1897, with revolutionary leaders having accepted exile outside of the country.

Lt. William Warren Kimball, Staff Intelligence Officer with the [Naval War College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_War_College "Naval War College")[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEDyalCarpenterThomas1996[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidCWaCEfeuQXkCpgPA180_180]-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEDyalCarpenterThomas1996[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidCWaCEfeuQXkCpgPA180_180]) prepared a plan for war with Spain including the Philippines on June 1, 1896, known as "the Kimball Plan".[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-112)

On April 23, 1898, a document from [Governor General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_the_Philippines "Governor-General of the Philippines") [Basilio Augustín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilio_August%C3%ADn "Basilio Augustín") appeared in the _Manila Gazette_ newspaper warning of the impending war and calling for Filipinos to participate on the side of Spain.[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[i\]](#refnote-117)

Roosevelt, who was at that time Assistant Secretary of the Navy, ordered Commodore [George Dewey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Dewey "George Dewey"), commanding the [Asiatic Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_Squadron "Asiatic Squadron") of the United States Navy: "Order the squadron ...to Hong Kong. Keep full of coal. In the event of declaration of war with Spain, your duty will be to see that the Spanish squadron does not leave the Asiatic coast, and then offensive operations in Philippine Islands." Dewey's squadron departed on April 27 for the Philippines, reaching [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay "Manila Bay") on the evening of April 30.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Howland1921-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-Howland1921)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/USS_Olympia_art_NH_91881-KN_cropped.jpg/250px-USS_Olympia_art_NH_91881-KN_cropped.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:USS_Olympia_art_NH_91881-KN_cropped.jpg) The [Battle of Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_Bay "Battle of Manila Bay")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Dotaci%C3%B3n_de_la_escuadrilla_en_la_bah%C3%ADa_de_Manila.jpg/250px-Dotaci%C3%B3n_de_la_escuadrilla_en_la_bah%C3%ADa_de_Manila.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dotaci%C3%B3n_de_la_escuadrilla_en_la_bah%C3%ADa_de_Manila.jpg) [Spanish Marines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Marines "Spanish Marines") entrenched during the [Battle of Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_Bay "Battle of Manila Bay")

The first battle between American and Spanish forces was at [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_Bay "Battle of Manila Bay") where, on May 1, [Commodore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commodore_(rank) "Commodore (rank)") Dewey, commanding the Asiatic Squadron aboard [USS _Olympia_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Olympia_(C-6) "USS Olympia (C-6)"), in a matter of hours defeated a Spanish [squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squadron_(naval) "Squadron (naval)") under Admiral [Patricio Montojo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patricio_Montojo "Patricio Montojo").[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[j\]](#refnote-120) Dewey managed this with only nine wounded.[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-121)[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-122) With the German seizure of [Qingdao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiaozhou_Bay_Leased_Territory "Jiaozhou Bay Leased Territory") in 1897, Dewey's squadron had become the only naval force in the Far East without a local base of its own, and was beset with coal and ammunition problems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AHRJune1988p659-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-AHRJune1988p659) Despite these problems, the Asiatic Squadron destroyed the Spanish fleet and captured Manila's harbor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AHRJune1988p659-1)[\[113\]](#refnote-AHRJune1988p659)

Following Dewey's victory, Manila Bay became filled with the warships of other naval powers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AHRJune1988p659-2)[\[113\]](#refnote-AHRJune1988p659) The German squadron of eight ships, ostensibly in Philippine waters to protect German interests, acted provocatively—cutting in front of American ships, refusing to salute the [American flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_flag "American flag") (according to customs of naval courtesy), taking soundings of the harbor, and landing supplies for the besieged Spanish.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-125)

With interests of their own, Germany was eager to take advantage of whatever opportunities the conflict in the islands might afford.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dolan1991-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-Dolan1991) There was a fear at the time that the islands would become a German possession.[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-127) The Americans called Germany's bluff and threatened conflict if the aggression continued. The Germans backed down.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Dolan1991-1)[\[116\]](#refnote-Dolan1991)[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-128) At the time, the Germans expected the confrontation in the Philippines to end in an American defeat, with the revolutionaries capturing Manila and leaving the Philippines ripe for German picking.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-129) The United States government had concerns about the self-government capability of the Filipinos, fearing that a power such as Germany or Japan might take control if the United States did not do so.[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-130)

Commodore Dewey transported [Emilio Aguinaldo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_Aguinaldo "Emilio Aguinaldo"), a Filipino leader who led rebellion against Spanish rule in the Philippines in 1896, from exile in Hong Kong to the Philippines to rally more Filipinos against the Spanish colonial government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-US_Library_of_Congress-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-US_Library_of_Congress) By June 9, Aguinaldo's forces controlled the provinces of [Bulacan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulacan "Bulacan"), [Cavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavite "Cavite"), [Laguna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laguna_(province) "Laguna (province)"), [Batangas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batangas "Batangas"), [Bataan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bataan "Bataan"), [Zambales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambales "Zambales"), [Pampanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pampanga "Pampanga"), [Pangasinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangasinan "Pangasinan"), and [Mindoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mindoro "Mindoro"), and had laid siege to Manila.[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-131) On June 12, Aguinaldo proclaimed the independence of the Philippines.[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-132)[&#8202;](#ref-back-pinas-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-pinas) While Aguinaldo's revolutionary forces were fighting, preparations were underway in the U.S. to send land forces to supplement Dewey's naval forces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FaustCh4-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-FaustCh4) Troops began departing San Francisco on May 25 and arriving on Manila on June 30,[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-135) having been officially formed as the U.S. Army the U.S. [Eighth Army Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_Army_Corps_(Spanish%E2%80%93American_War) "Eighth Army Corps (Spanish–American War)") on June 21 while they were in transit.

On August 5, upon instruction from Spain, Governor-General [Basilio Augustin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilio_Augustin "Basilio Augustin") turned over the command of the Philippines to his deputy, [Fermin Jaudenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fermin_Jaudenes "Fermin Jaudenes").[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-136) On August 13, with American commanders unaware that a peace protocol had been signed between Spain and the U.S. on the previous day in Washington D.C., American forces captured the city of Manila from the Spanish in the [Battle of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_(1898) "Battle of Manila (1898)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Manila_Protocol_13-0)[\[c\]](#refnote-Manila_Protocol_13)[&#8202;](#ref-back-US_Library_of_Congress-1)[\[10\]](#refnote-US_Library_of_Congress)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Flag_Manila-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-Flag_Manila) This battle marked the end of Filipino–American collaboration, as the American action of preventing Filipino forces from entering the captured city of Manila was deeply resented by the Filipinos. This later led to the [Philippine–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine%E2%80%93American_War "Philippine–American War"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-lac126-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-lac126) which would prove to be more deadly and costly than the Spanish–American War.

The U.S. had sent a force of some 11,000 ground troops to the Philippines. On August 14, 1898, Spanish Captain-General Jaudenes formally capitulated. U.S. General [Wesley Merritt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wesley_Merritt "Wesley Merritt") formally accepted the surrender and declared the establishment of a U.S. military government in occupation. The capitulation document declared, "The surrender of the Philippine Archipelago." and set forth a mechanism for its physical accomplishment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEForeman1906[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidW2crAAAAYAAJdq22The20surrender20of20the20Philippine20Archipelago22pgPA465_465]-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEForeman1906[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidW2crAAAAYAAJdq22The20surrender20of20the20Philippine20Archipelago22pgPA465_465])[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-139) That same day, the [Schurman Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schurman_Commission "Schurman Commission") recommended that the U.S. retain control of the Philippines, possibly granting independence in the future.[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-140) On December 10, 1898, the Spanish government ceded the Philippines to the United States in the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1898) "Treaty of Paris (1898)"). Armed conflict broke out between U.S. forces and the Filipinos when U.S. troops began to take the place of the Spanish in control of the country after the end of the war, quickly escalating into the Philippine–American War.

### Guam

Main article: [Capture of Guam (1898)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Guam_(1898) "Capture of Guam (1898)")

On June 20, 1898, the [protected cruiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_cruiser "Protected cruiser") [USS _Charleston_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Charleston_(C-2) "USS Charleston (C-2)") commanded by Captain [Henry Glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Glass_(admiral) "Henry Glass (admiral)"), and three transports carrying troops to the Philippines, entered Guam's Apra Harbor. Captain Glass had opened sealed orders instructing him to proceed to [Guam and capture it](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Guam_(1898) "Capture of Guam (1898)") while en route to the Philippines. _Charleston_ fired a few rounds at the abandoned Fort Santa Cruz without receiving return fire. Two local officials, not knowing that war had been declared and believing the firing had been a salute, came out to _Charleston_ to apologize for their inability to return the salute as they were out of gunpowder. Glass informed them that the U.S. and Spain were at war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208–209-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-208–209)

The following day, Glass sent Lieutenant William Braunersreuther to meet the Spanish Governor to arrange the surrender of the island and the Spanish garrison there. Two officers, 54 Spanish infantrymen as well as the governor-general and his staff were taken prisoner\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] and transported to the Philippines as prisoners of war. No U.S. forces were left on Guam, but the only U.S. citizen on the island, [Frank Portusach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Portusach "Frank Portusach"), told Captain Glass that he would look after things until U.S. forces returned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208–209-1)[\[131\]](#refnote-208–209)

## Caribbean theater

### Cuba

See also: [San Juan Hill order of battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_Hill_order_of_battle "San Juan Hill order of battle") and [El Caney order of battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Caney_order_of_battle "El Caney order of battle")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/67/Cristobal-colon_h63229.jpg/250px-Cristobal-colon_h63229.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cristobal-colon_h63229.jpg) The Spanish armored cruiser [_Cristóbal Colón_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_cruiser_Crist%C3%B3bal_Col%C3%B3n "Spanish cruiser Cristóbal Colón"), which was destroyed during the Battle of Santiago on July 3, 1898[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/San_Juan_Hill_by_Kurz_and_Allison.JPG/250px-San_Juan_Hill_by_Kurz_and_Allison.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:San_Juan_Hill_by_Kurz_and_Allison.JPG) Detail from _Charge of the [24th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24th_Infantry_Regiment_(United_States) "24th Infantry Regiment (United States)") and [25th Colored Infantry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/25th_Infantry_Regiment_(United_States) "25th Infantry Regiment (United States)") and Rescue of Rough Riders at San Juan Hill, July 2, 1898_, depicting the [Battle of San Juan Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_San_Juan_Hill "Battle of San Juan Hill")

Theodore Roosevelt advocated intervention in Cuba, both for the Cuban people and to promote the Monroe Doctrine. While Assistant Secretary of the Navy, he placed the Navy on a war-time footing and prepared Dewey's Asiatic Squadron for battle. He also worked with [Leonard Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonard_Wood "Leonard Wood") in convincing the Army to raise an all-volunteer regiment, the 1st U.S. Volunteer Cavalry. Wood was given command of the regiment that quickly became known as the " [Rough Riders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rough_Riders "Rough Riders")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-142)

The Americans planned to destroy Spain's army forces in Cuba, capture the port city of Santiago de Cuba, and destroy the Spanish Caribbean Squadron (also known as the Flota de Ultramar). To reach Santiago they had to pass through concentrated Spanish defenses in the San Juan Hills and a small town in [El Caney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Caney "El Caney"). The American forces were aided in Cuba by the pro-independence rebels led by General [Calixto García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calixto_Garc%C3%ADa "Calixto García").

#### Cuban sentiment

For quite some time the Cuban public believed the United States government to possibly hold the key to its independence, and even annexation was considered for a time, which historian Louis Pérez explored in his book _Cuba and the United States: Ties of Singular Intimacy_. The Cubans harbored a great deal of discontent towards the Spanish government, a result of years of manipulation on the part of the Spanish. The prospect of getting the United States involved in the fight was considered by many Cubans as a step in the right direction. While the Cubans were wary of the United States' intentions, the overwhelming support from the American public provided the Cubans with some peace of mind, because they believed that the United States was committed to helping them achieve their independence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-143)

#### Action at Cienfuegos

The first combat between American and Spanish forces in the Caribbean occurred on May 11, 1898, in the harbor near the city of [Cienfuegos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cienfuegos "Cienfuegos").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-:5) The city was the southern terminus for undersea communication cables that connected Cuba to Spain and other Spanish holdings in the Caribbean. American Naval officers needed to destroy these cables to cut communications into and out of Cuba, in preparation for later operations against the major city of Santiago.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-:6) The [USS _Marblehead_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Marblehead_(C-11) "USS Marblehead (C-11)") and the [USS _Nashville_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Nashville_(PG-7) "USS Nashville (PG-7)") were dispatched to cut these cables early on the morning of May 11. To cut the cables, two steam cutters, with a crew of eight sailors and six Marines each, and two sailing launches, with a crew of fourteen sailors each, maneuvered into harbor and within 200 feet from shore.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-1)[\[135\]](#refnote-:6)

While the boats moved towards shore, the _Marblehead_ and _Nashville_ shelled Spanish trenches dug to protect the cables from sabotage attempts. They succeeded in destroying support buildings for the cables and drove the Spanish force back away from the beach. The boats' crews pulled one cable up and began trying to cut through its metal jacket while Spanish soldiers started firing from cover. Marine sharpshooters returned fire from the boats and the _Marblehead_ and _Nashville_ began firing shrapnel shells in an attempt to force the Spanish completely out of the area.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-2)[\[135\]](#refnote-:6) The sailors finished cutting one cable and pulled up a second one to begin severing it too. Spanish fire began to take a toll on the Marines and sailors with multiple casualties in the small boats, but the Americans were still able to cut a second wire. They began working on the final wire and succeeded in partially cutting it until the still heavy Spanish fire and mounting casualties forced the Navy officer in command, Lieutenant E. A. Anderson, to order the boats to return to the cover of the larger vessels.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-3)[\[135\]](#refnote-:6)

In the almost three hours of combat, two men were killed, two mortally wounded, and four more seriously wounded and they succeeded in severing two of the three cables running out of Cienfuegos.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-:7) Contemporary press reported that among the wounded, Lieutenant Winslow lost three fingers of his left hand, and Captain Maynard of the Nashville was struck by a glancing shell fragment to the head; the named casualties included Gunners Robert Boltz, John Davis, John Doran, Earnest Suntzench, Herman W. Kohmaster, and I. Hedrickson, with Private Egan among the killed. Boltz and Hedrickson were reported as mortally wounded. The strategic consequence was immediately recognized: cutting the cable "practically shuts out Blanco from communication with the outside world," leaving the Spanish commander able to wire only from Santiago over the English cable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-147) This relatively brief fight significantly disrupted communications between Cuba, Santiago, and Spain and contributed to the overall American goal of isolating Cuba from outside support. It also provided a major boost to American morale because it was the first combat American servicemen had seen close to home. Captain McCall's official after-action report, made public by the Navy Department on May 18, confirmed that four boats participated under Lieutenant Sutherland commanding the Eagle and Lieutenant Winslow commanding the steam launches, with Lieutenant A.E. Anderson second in command. A small in-shore cable was located but could not be grappled due to an infantry force with a Maxim gun positioned at only 150 yards; the enemy eventually concealed themselves in a lighthouse and opened fire, at which point the ships' guns destroyed it. McCall wrote of the crews: "I cannot speak in too high praise of officers and men engaged in the four boats in cutting the cables. Their work was perfected with the utmost coolness and intrepidity, under the most trying circumstances."[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-148) For their brave actions, all the Marines and sailors in the four small boats received the [Medal of Honor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_of_Honor "Medal of Honor").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-1)[\[136\]](#refnote-:7)

#### Land campaign

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/5/53/Spanish_Mauser_1893.png/250px-Spanish_Mauser_1893.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Spanish_Mauser_1893.png) The [Mauser Model 1893](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauser_Model_1893 "Mauser Model 1893") rifle, used by the Spanish infantry and perceived to be superior to the Springfield Model 1892–99 used by the [U.S. Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army "United States Army").[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-149) Because of this, the U.S. Army later developed the [M1903 Springfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1903_Springfield "M1903 Springfield").[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[k\]](#refnote-150)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/America%27s_war_for_humanity_related_in_story_and_picture%2C_embracing_a_complete_history_of_Cuba%27s_struggle_for_liberty%2C_and_the_glorious_heroism_of_America%27s_soldiers_and_sailors_%281898%29_%2814591603718%29.jpg/250px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:America's_war_for_humanity_related_in_story_and_picture,_embracing_a_complete_history_of_Cuba's_struggle_for_liberty,_and_the_glorious_heroism_of_America's_soldiers_and_sailors_(1898)_(14591603718).jpg) Charge of the [Rough Riders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rough_Riders "Rough Riders")[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Charge_of_the_Rough_Riders_at_San_Juan_Hill.JPG/250px-Charge_of_the_Rough_Riders_at_San_Juan_Hill.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Charge_of_the_Rough_Riders_at_San_Juan_Hill.JPG) The Rough Riders in San Juan ( [Frederic Remington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederic_Remington "Frederic Remington"))

The first American landings in Cuba occurred on June 10 with the landing of the First Marine Battalion at Fisherman's Point in [Guantánamo Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guant%C3%A1namo_Bay "Guantánamo Bay").[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-151) This was followed on June 22 to 24, when the [Fifth Army Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Army_Corps_(Spanish%E2%80%93American_War) "Fifth Army Corps (Spanish–American War)") under General [William R. Shafter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_R._Shafter "William R. Shafter") landed at [Daiquirí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daiquir%C3%AD "Daiquirí") and [Siboney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siboney,_Cuba "Siboney, Cuba"), east of Santiago, and established an American base of operations. A contingent of Spanish troops, having fought a skirmish with the Americans near Siboney on June 23, had retired to their lightly entrenched positions at [Las Guasimas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Las_Guasimas "Battle of Las Guasimas"). An advance guard of U.S. forces under former [Confederate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_Army "Confederate States Army") General [Joseph Wheeler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Wheeler "Joseph Wheeler") ignored Cuban scouting parties and orders to proceed with caution. They caught up with and engaged the Spanish rearguard of about 2,000 soldiers led by General [Antero Rubín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antero_Rub%C3%ADn "Antero Rubín")[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-152) who effectively ambushed them, in the Battle of Las Guasimas on June 24. The battle ended indecisively in favor of Spain and the Spanish left Las Guasimas on their planned retreat to Santiago.

The U.S. Army employed Civil War–era [skirmishers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skirmisher#American_Civil_War "Skirmisher") at the head of the advancing columns. Three of four of the U.S. soldiers who had volunteered to act as skirmishers walking point at the head of the American column were killed, including [Hamilton Fish II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamilton_Fish_II_(Rough_Rider) "Hamilton Fish II (Rough Rider)") (grandson of [Hamilton Fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamilton_Fish "Hamilton Fish"), the Secretary of State under Ulysses S. Grant), and Captain [Allyn K. Capron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allyn_K._Capron,_Jr. "Allyn K.
